Many harnesses. One scripted model. Rerun it on yours.

Buffon measures coding-agent harnesses against the same scripted model, so a number describes the harness and not a model. Every cell carries its unit, its spread and the command that measures it again.

The grade and faithfulness

One score per entry from the families below, and a separate faithfulness score for how closely it behaves like Pi. Faithfulness is never a weight in the score. The method is fixed before a run and is the same for every entry.

Run notes

4 notes: aa_significant_fraction 5.4%, aa_gap_max 25.1%, aa_floor_max 700.4%, outlier_fraction 2.6%. Run notes describe the conditions of the run. They do not grade it or change a number.

checkvalueusual rangestatusmeaning
A/A pairs with a significant gap (paired p < 0.05, before Holm) that is also larger than 1%5.4%up to 5.0%noteShare of harness and copy pairs that differ by more than 1% and also pass a significance test. A gap under 1% on a steady counter is not counted. About 5% can happen by chance. overhead/print-write anthropic kb200_peak_mem_mib rpi: 25.1%; overhead/print-write anthropic kb200_peak_rss_mib rpi: 22.4%; overhead/print-write chat kb50_after_stream_ms opencode-v2: 16.2%
A/A gap between a harness and its own copy, largest cell25.1%up to 5.0%noteThe largest gap between a harness and its identical copy, and the cell where it happened. overhead/print-write anthropic kb200_peak_mem_mib rpi: 25.1%; overhead/print-write anthropic kb200_peak_rss_mib rpi: 22.4%; overhead/print-write chat kb50_after_stream_ms opencode-v2: 16.2%
A/A noise floor, worst scored cell700.4%up to 15.0%noteThe same floor in the noisiest cell. Comparisons in that cell resolve only larger differences. streaming/print-prose anthropic n10_peak_rss_mib: 700.4%; overhead/print-write anthropic kb200_peak_mem_mib: 38.9%; overhead/print-write chat kb50_after_stream_ms: 37.8%
Measured values flagged by the MAD rule2.6%up to 2.0%noteShare of measured values far from the median of their cell. Outliers are counted and never removed. overhead/print-write chat kb50_cpu_ms pi-node-b: 3; overhead/print-write chat kb50_cycles pi-node-b: 3; overhead/print-write chat kb50_wall_ms pi-node-b: 3
Calibration: CV of the reference's CPU time between identical runs1.4%up to 2.0%okHow much the reference's CPU time varied between identical calibration runs. Lower means a quieter host.
A/A CV of CPU metrics, median over arms and cellsn/aup to 2.0%okHow much CPU time varies from round to round for one harness. Lower means a quieter host.
A/A gap between a harness and its own copy, median over cells0.4%up to 1.0%okThe usual gap between a harness and its identical copy. A real difference between two harnesses should be larger than this.
A/A noise floor, median over scored cells1.8%up to 3.0%okThe smallest difference the identical copies cannot tell from noise, in the usual cell. Smaller differences than this are not resolved.
Measured samples with at least one host flag2.3%up to 5.0%okShare of samples where the host showed a disturbance: throttling, foreign CPU, steal, a clock change, swap or a memory event.
Measured samples with CFS throttling (arm cgroup, every ancestor, session)0up to 0okSamples where the kernel held the process back for a CPU quota. Their time then describes the quota, not the harness.
Steal: share of the pinned CPUs' capacity taken by a hypervisor0.0%up to 0.1%okShare of the pinned CPUs' time that a hypervisor took away. It only matters on virtual machines.
Foreign CPU on the pinned CPUs during a sample, p95 over samples, as a share of the sample's CPU time0.0%up to 1.0%okCPU time that other processes used on the pinned cores during a sample, as a share of the sample's own CPU time (95th percentile).
Samples that waited more than 1 s for the host to quiet down0.0%up to 1.0%okShare of samples that had to wait for the quiet-host gate. A busy host makes the gate wait.
Samples for which the host never quieted down0up to 0okSamples that did not start because the host stayed busy. They are not in the numbers.
Effective clock: largest deviation of a sample's mean MHz from the run median0.2%up to 2.0%okHow far a sample's mean CPU clock moved from the run median. A moving clock changes CPU time for the same work.
Infrastructure failures (envelope OOM, no process started, runner fault) over measured samples0.0%up to 0.0%okShare of samples that failed because of the host or the runner. They are never charged to a harness.
Arm failures over measured samples (a swarm ladder's designed stops are results and not counted)0.0%up to 2.0%okShare of samples where the harness itself failed. A scale sample that stopped because the kernel's limit was reached (stop_cause oom, pids or timeout) is a result and is in neither the failures nor the samples counted; a scale error stop is a failure.
Drift: median absolute change over the run of an arm's value, over cells0.5%up to 2.0%okHow much a harness's value moved from the start of the run to the end. Drift means the host changed during the run.
No change point in the reference arm's per-round seriesyesyesokThe host's speed did not shift during the run. A shift moves every comparison the same way.
At least 12 measured roundsyesyesokWith fewer rounds the intervals are wide.
Every arm has an A/A copyyesyesokWithout a copy there is no noise floor for that harness.
Quiet dedicated hardware by the runner's own detectionyesyesokOn shared hardware other work can disturb the numbers.
No harness connected outside the loopbackyesyesokA harness that talks to the network is not measured alone.
Every executable and install tree unchanged during the runyesyesokA changed executable means the arm is not what the lock names.
Calibration ran and the reference stayed within its 5% limityesyesokCalibration shows the host's noise before the run. Above 5% the run is labelled Noisy.
Telemetry recorded for every measured sampleyesyesokTelemetry is how the other checks see the host.
No CPU quota (cpu.max) on the arm cgroup or any ancestoryesyesokA quota can throttle a sample.
The pinned CPUs have a fixed clock policyyesyesokA clock that scales with load changes CPU time for the same work.

The usual ranges are published in docs/HEALTH.md and are the same for every run. The notes are recomputed from the raw data by buffon verify; they are not stored in raw.json. The A/A rows compare each harness with its own identical copy. A pair counts as different only when its gap is significant and also larger than 1%.

A score of 70 is the reference's; each halving of cost in a family is +10 points and each doubling -10. A letter is the school band of the rounded score (A 90, B 80, C 70, D 60, F below; + and - at the top and bottom 3 points). Run notes: 4.

A/A noise floor

Each arm's A/A copy <id>-b is the same executable run in the same rounds. The floor of a cell is the largest deviation from 1 the copy's ratio-of-medians interval allows; a difference between arms smaller than the floor is reported as no measurable difference. The table gives, per arm and workload, the median and the maximum floor over the scored metrics, and the reference's minimum detectable effect at 80% power.

Cell ids and rerun commands

Every cell has an id, <run id>::<workload>@<api>#<metric>. python3 -m buffon rerun <cell id> --site <your site profile> repeats the plan of the run that holds the cell (its arms, rounds, seed and parameters) for that workload and API only, on your host, and compares your result with the original. The raw samples of every cell are in the run directory's raw.json; this report is generated from them and from nothing else, and buffon verify regenerates it to check that no number was edited.

A/A gap: the copy's median against the arm's, per cell. A/A floor: the largest deviation from 1 the copy's ratio interval allows (METHODOLOGY section 6). CV: the standard deviation of an arm's rounds over their mean. Outliers: a measured value is an outlier when |x - median| > 5 x 1.4826 x MAD of its arm's values in that cell (needs 4 values; with MAD 0, any different value). Outliers are counted, never removed. Drift: the Theil-Sen trend of an arm's rounds times the rounds spanned, as a share of its median.

Run 1

  • Samples: 384 measured (384 ok, 0 failed), 52 warm-up; telemetry on 384 of 384 ok samples. Sources read: cgroup_throttle, cpufreq, loadavg, meminfo, pressure, proc_stat, schedstat, thermal_throttle, vmstat.
  • Calibration: CV 1.4% of cpu_ms on pi-node (gate 5.0%), passed.
  • CFS throttling: 0 samples, 0 us in all; the session's cgroup lost 0 us over the whole run.
  • Quiet-host gate: 384 passed at once, 0 waited (longest 0.0 s, 0.0 s in all), 0 gave up.
  • Steal: 0.000% of the pinned CPUs' capacity. Foreign CPU on the pinned CPUs: p95 0.0% of the sample's CPU time. Clock: largest deviation 0.2% from the median 2800 MHz. iowait is recorded in every sample and never gated.
  • Failed samples: 0 arm, 0 infrastructure (the arm is not charged for those); 0 ladder stops (a swarm rung that hit the kernel's limit: a result, not a failure). CPU quota on the arm cgroup or an ancestor: none.
  • Flagged samples: 9 of 384 (clock_moved 9).
  • Telemetry cost: the collector's thread used 19.35 ms median per sample (worst 265.72 ms), on the runner's CPUs, outside the pinned set.
  • Host load before / after: 1.0 / 1.3; pinned CPUs busy over the run: mean 13.4%, max 32.7%.

Samples and rounds not in the statistics

reasonsamplesrounds
warm-up round (kept in the raw data, excluded from the statistics by rule)521

Flags (buffon/report/thresholds.py):

  • throttled: CFS throttled the sample's cgroup, an ancestor or the session (1 period or more)
  • foreign_busy: the pinned CPUs were busy beyond the sample's own CPU time by more than 3% of their capacity (and 2 clock ticks per sqrt(CPU))
  • steal: a hypervisor took more than 0.1% of the pinned CPUs' capacity
  • clock_moved: the pinned CPUs' clock moved by more than 5% during the sample
  • gate_waited: the quiet-host gate needed more than one reading before the sample started
  • swap: the host swapped 1 page or more during the sample
  • thermal: the pinned CPUs were thermally throttled 1 time or more during the sample
  • memory_events: the kernel reported a memory.high, memory.max or OOM event for the sample's cgroup

Egress

Method: socket-audit. Any connection to a non-loopback address fails the sample and the run. No harness connected outside the loopback.

Not measured

Projects that resemble Pi implementations and were left out, with the reason:

projectreason
chinudotdev/pi-goa Go library for building agents, not a runnable coding agent: there is no command to start, so there is nothing to put through a workload
qiz029/pi-agent-goa Go SDK (client) for Pi's RPC protocol: it drives a Pi process, it is not an implementation of Pi; measuring it would measure Pi
rcarmo/go-aian inference API library (the model layer under rcarmo/gi), not a coding agent

Runtime settings

Buffon pins CPUs but sets no runtime tuning of its own. An arm runs at its harness's shipped default except for the variables its manifest sets:

armset by the manifestleft at the harness default

Quiet dedicated hardware: generated from 1 run directory by buffon report; 4 notes: aa_significant_fraction 5.4%, aa_gap_max 25.1%, aa_floor_max 700.4%, outlier_fraction 2.6%; the raw samples are in each directory's raw.json. The same data as report.html, report.json, summary.csv, rounds.csv, noise.csv and telemetry.csv.

Drift: per-round series

The rounds themselves, in the order they ran. Amount axes start at zero; the A/A ratio axis is centred on 1. A line that climbs or falls over the run is the host drifting; the data is in rounds.csv.

Host during the run

96 more cells have their series in plots/ (one SVG each) and in rounds.csv.

Limitationswhat a number cannot tell you

Limitations

What a Buffon number can and cannot tell you. Read this page before you compare two numbers. Each limitation says what is limited, how it changes the way to read a number, and what the report does about it.

None of these makes a result wrong. They say what question a result answers. WHAT-WE-MEASURE.md gives the limits specific to each family, and METHODOLOGY.md says how the numbers are produced.

1. A mock model is not a network

The mock model replies at once, with no network latency and no provider variance. A real turn is dominated by model time and network time.

Reading a number: it describes what the harness adds to a turn, and not the time a person waits. Where a real model is used: only in the opt-in field track, which is planned and is kept out of the grade.

2. Mock token counts are estimates

The mock reports prompt tokens as the request's wire bytes divided by 4, and completion tokens from the stream. They are not a provider's counts. A harness that sends a larger request sees a larger count and compacts earlier. That is part of what is measured.

Reading a number: a difference in compaction behaviour can come from request size and does not show a difference in how well a harness compacts. The report: records the wire bytes of every request, so a reader can see the size that drove a count.

3. One machine, one kernel

A run comes from one host, with one CPU model, kernel, frequency governor and terminal. Another host can give other values.

Reading a number: it holds for the host in the report. The A/A floor and the minimum detectable effect state how noisy this run was on this host, and neither removes the host. The report: records the host and labels a run that is not on quiet dedicated hardware PROVISIONAL. An independent rerun on a second host is required before any public release.

Slots (opt-in): with [local] slots, several sessions share one host and one kernel. The slot calibration shows that the reference arm's CPU and wall time did not move on its workloads; it does not show the same for a family that stresses the shared last-level cache, memory bandwidth or disk harder. Each session's own A/A floor and quiet check are where such interference would show. A slotted run is one more reason to rerun on a host that runs one session at a time before a public release.

4. Memory means different things

"Memory" names four readings, and each answers a different question. A report gives each its own column and names it.

kindwhat it counts
own footprintmemory this process holds that no other process shares
RSSthe pages of this process that are in RAM now, shared libraries and shared pages included
peak RSSthe largest RSS the process reached
heap in usewhat the runtime itself reports using, where the arm exposes it

Footprint can sit far below RSS, because RSS counts shared pages. A runtime with a lazy garbage collector can hold memory it no longer needs, and a reading right after an answer differs from the reading at rest. So each sampled kind is read at fixed times after the last answer (30 ms, 2 s and 10 s), and a report names the kind and the time of every memory column (REPORT-TEMPLATE.md). Today a run reports peak RSS and PSS samples. Own footprint, heap in use and the three times are planned.

Why a macOS footprint depends on the allocator

On macOS, own footprint is ri_phys_footprint, read through proc_pid_rusage. It is a figure the kernel keeps per process. On Linux the matching figure is Private_Dirty from smaps_rollup, the private pages the process has modified. Pages of a file on a memory-backed file system (tmpfs, /dev/shm) are shared memory, and a mapping of one counts as dirty: an executable, a runtime image or a cache that runs from a tmpfs directory adds its whole text to Private_Dirty. A footprint number is like-for-like only when every arm's code runs from disk; the container envelope's HOME and TMPDIR are tmpfs (METHODOLOGY Run Rule 9), so a footprint column must state where each arm's executable and runtime images were mapped from. Both aim at "memory only this process holds", through different kernel accounting, so they are not the same quantity.

An allocator on macOS can tell the kernel that it has finished with pages (MADV_FREE_REUSABLE) and later that it uses them again (MADV_FREE_REUSE). The footprint of a process can depend on whether the allocator makes the second call. A comment in the allocator mimalloc, in the fork kept by the oven-sh organisation, explains why that fork does not make it:

Deliberately do NOT call MADV_FREE_REUSE. The kernel charges pages to phys_footprint as they are touched, but the explicit REUSE re-charges the ENTIRE range up front -- including OS pages the program never touches again.

The same comment says that issuing the call would have changed a measurement its authors made on a churn workload "24MB -> 161MB", and that libpas and bmalloc, the allocators of WebKit, do not issue it on Darwin for the same reason.
repositoryoven-sh/mimalloc
commit92ef6587c57b262f6e6abf513f7776a2f8d12aeb (92ef658, 2026-10-04)
file and linessrc/prim/unix/prim.c, lines 554-567, the function _mi_prim_reuse
sha256 of those 14 lines9c14851be044c9edb60ff077df267511b820a8995bb41374a42d000cfe822fde
checked2026-10-13, by fetching the file at that commit

The quotation is from MIT-licensed source. The figure is one measurement by the allocator's authors on one workload. It is not a general figure, and Buffon has not measured how large the effect is for any arm.

Reading a number:

  • A macOS footprint compares two runtimes partly through how each one's allocator treats this call, as well as through how much memory each one uses. A difference between two macOS footprints is not by itself a difference in memory use.
  • Linux Private_Dirty is the like-for-like figure for comparing harnesses by own footprint. MADV_FREE_REUSE is a macOS call, so this effect is absent there. Linux allocators still differ in how eagerly they return memory, and that remains part of what the column measures.
  • Read RSS and peak RSS beside a macOS footprint, and do not compare a macOS number with a Linux number.
  • Which allocator a runtime uses is a fact of that runtime and version. Buffon does not assume it from the runtime's name.

Peak memory of all processes on macOS

The "peak memory, all processes" figure of a macOS run is the largest sum of resident sets over ps samples. A sample takes tens to hundreds of milliseconds, so a command that runs for less than that may leave no sample, or only one taken right after its exec. With no sample, the figure is missing and the report has no cell for it. With one, the figure is raised to the largest single process's ru_maxrss, so it can still read low for a tree of several processes that peaked together. The largest-process peak (peak_rss_mib) comes from wait4 and does not have this gap. Earlier runs of Buffon printed a near-zero figure for such commands; one start-up cell read 2121 times the reference because of it.

Reading a number: a macOS all-processes peak for a command of under a second is a lower bound. Prefer the largest-process peak there, and do not set a macOS cell against a Linux cgroup peak.

The report: labels the meter of a run (cgroup or rusage). The planned memory table names the platform in its note and keeps macOS and Linux rows apart (REPORT-TEMPLATE.md).

5. Some metrics favour a design

A harness that deduplicates repeated content gains on a workload that repeats content, and the long-session workloads repeat a pattern. A harness that defers work until first use looks cheap at start and pays on the first prompt. A harness that sends less context does less work per turn. None of these is a statement about quality.

Reading a number: ask what design the metric rewards before you read a difference as a difference in quality. Where it is explained: here, and in the limits of each family in WHAT-WE-MEASURE.md. The long-session resume workloads have a varied twin (longsession/resume-n-varied, resume-n10-varied) with distinct content, the same message structure and the same byte size, so a deduplication gain shows as the difference between the two rows. The other workloads have no twin. A per-row note in the generated report is planned.

6. Defaults differ and are not equalised

Arms are configured through their manifests to run the same scenario. Each keeps its own compaction defaults, tool set and system prompt. A different prompt is a different request, and a different request changes what the mock reports.

Runtime tuning is one of those defaults. Buffon pins CPUs but sets no GOMAXPROCS, GOGC, GOMEMLIMIT or NODE_OPTIONS for any arm, so a Go harness sizes itself by its own runtime (PiG documents GOMAXPROCS unset, capped at min(2, the runtime default)). Under a one-core cpuset Go's default follows the cpuset, so the difference shows only on a run with several harness CPUs.

Where a harness does different work by its own account (Pi-Bolt parses a streaming tool call's arguments less often than Pi and scrolls rows in the fullscreen TUI), the report labels the cell "different work: not compared" and the grade leaves it out for that arm.

Reading a number: the comparison is between harnesses as they ship, configured to run the same scenario, and not between harnesses with identical settings. A cell labelled "different work: not compared" has no ratio at all. The report: prints the pins, the environment and the runtime settings of every arm, and the declared divergences with their sources.

7. Buffon's authors also build PiG, and PiG is one of the arms

Reading a number: weigh a PiG result with that in mind. The report: METHODOLOGY.md section 15 lists what limits the effect: the same code scores every arm, the raw samples ship with every run, the losses are listed first, any cell can be rerun by someone else, and an independent rerun on a second host comes before any public release.

8. Repetitions are few

A cell has at least 12 measured repetitions per arm, and more where the A/A floor says the cell is noisy. A cell with few repetitions has a wide interval.

With adaptive stopping a cell can take fewer than 12 rounds, and cannot be Verified. It never takes fewer than the rounds Holm across its compared arms needs for the exact sign-flip test to reach p < 0.05: 6 for one arm, 9 for eight. Few rounds also limit equivalence: the TOST needs a 90% interval inside the margin, which a handful of noisy rounds rarely gives, so a cell can show "no measurable difference" and "not equivalent" together.

Reading a number: look at the interval and the A/A floor, and not only at the median. The report: prints the interval, the spread and the minimum detectable effect, and calls a gap inside the floor "no measurable difference".

9. Terminals differ

An interactive run uses a fixed PTY size and one of two terminal profiles. A harness can behave differently on a terminal that answers queries differently.

Reading a number: an interactive number holds for the profile named in the report. The report: names the profile and reports the two profiles separately.

10. Platforms differ

A Linux run measures CPU and memory from a cgroup. A macOS run uses rusage and ps, and the report labels it as rusage. It also lacks some cells a Linux run has (RPC and long-session peak RSS) and reads some CPU values from live readings that miss exited helpers; see METHODOLOGY section 4, "Measurement caveats by platform".

On macOS the all-processes peak memory of a short command may be missing or a lower bound (section 4), and its sampler has a longer interval than the Linux one.

Reading a number: do not compare a Linux number with a macOS number. The report: labels the meter and keeps the two apart.

11. Instruction counts depend on the host's counters

On Linux a strict perf_event_paranoid setting counts user-space instructions only, and a time-shared counter gives a scaled estimate. On macOS the count is sampled about every 165 ms (on the machine audited) and undercounts: a process that exits between two samples misses its last interval, and the shortfall depends on how long the process lives (about 44% in one test loop). Where an instruction cell exists the grade uses it instead of CPU time, so a macOS grade inherits the undercount (METHODOLOGY section 4, "Measurement caveats by platform"). A host with no counters reports CPU time only.

Reading a number: check which instructions were counted, and check a macOS number against a Linux host before relying on it. The report: each sample records which instructions were counted (perf_scope).

12. Faithfulness is graded by probes Buffon wrote

The probes cover what FAITHFULNESS.md lists and nothing else. A harness can differ from Pi in a way no probe exercises.

Reading a number: a faithfulness score is a statement about the probes, and not about every behaviour. The report: lists the probes that differ and the harness's recorded intentional divergences by name.

13. Not measured

Real model quality, a real network, Windows, and leaks that take hours to show. Families that have no workload yet are listed in WHAT-WE-MEASURE.md as not run.

The report: has a "Not measured" section.

Buffon
